91,2026-07-29,"Officials warn homeowners to watch out for those who say they can help.
For more than two decades, Fabiana Polizzi lived in a house just a block from the water on New York's Staten Island. But as she walked through her living room on a recent afternoon, there was hardly anything left inside.
""It becomes reality to watch your whole life being loaded into trucks, and it's worse when it's not because you want to,"" Polizzi said.
It was a day she feared was coming for a long time. Polizzi, a single mother of an 8-year-old boy and two 17-year-old girls, was joining a growing number of Americans going through the foreclosure process.
""It's hard because as a parent you're supposed to protect your kids and I feel like I failed them,"" Polizzi told ABC News.
Polizzi looked toward the stairwell leading up toward her children's soon-to-be former bedrooms. She said her daughters seemed to be overwhelmed with the unplanned move to a rental home in a different neighborhood.
""They thought they were going to come down those stairs in their wedding dresses,"" Polizzi said.
Trying to bring down her mortgage payment to stay out of foreclosure, Polizzi said she previously turned to a local company to help her get a loan modification -- but the modification never came.
Instead of getting the financial help she thought would help save her home, the house went into foreclosure. An eviction notice arrived later.
In a letter to a Richmond County judge, Polizzi wrote, ""This is not just a financial transaction. This is our home. This is our life.""
Polizzi's foreclosure experience comes as foreclosures around the United States are on the rise.
An analysis by ABC News and ABC Owned Television Stations of ATTOM data found that nationwide, foreclosure filings spiked 21% in the first six months of 2026 compared to the same time last year.
""For most people, their home is the rock of their economic security plan,"" University of Minnesota Law School professor Prentiss Cox said. ""Having that taken away, and having the last bits of equity that they would have accumulated taken away, is just devastating.""
During the COVID-19 pandemic, there were fewer foreclosures because of programs to help people stay in their homes. With many of those programs gone and the cost of living increasing, foreclosures are increasing.
""When there's a foreclosure filing, that's actually public record,"" Chase Haller, Homeowner Protection Unit Section Chief with the Indiana Attorney General's Office, told ABC affiliate WRTV. ""A scammer can get that information very easily.""
For Vanessa Lewry, the stress of going through the foreclosure process is nothing new. Lewry's father spent his retirement in a house he owned in Mesa, Arizona.
""We used to go down there all the time,"" Lewry, who's from Canada, explained. ""I'd go visit my family, and vacation there.""
Lewry said that as her dad battled dementia a decade ago, she found out that the house was in the process of being foreclosed on. She soon realized there was even more to the situation when she began receiving phone calls at work from a man telling her that he was her father's new landlord.
""I said, 'What are you talking about?'"" Lewry said.
According to Lewry, a company run by a man named Cameron Jones that called itself ""Arizona's Helping Hands"" found out the home was in foreclosure. Soon, Jones showed up at Lewry's father's front door and convinced him to sign over the property, Lewry said.
Lewry took Jones to court, fighting to get her father's house back. She says they ultimately reached an agreement where Jones did not admit wrongdoing, but between the loss of the house and attorney's fees, she says she believes the family lost between $100,000 and $150,000.
""Even until he died, he was in the hospital bed and he said, 'Get my house back.' And I said, 'I'll do my best,'"" Lewry told ABC News with tears in her eyes.
Jones is now facing a lawsuit from the Arizona Attorney General's Office alleging that he and his co-defendants ran a ""racketeering enterprise"" impacting hundreds of other homeowners. 
""The scheme starts with the scammers figuring out that somebody is in financial distress,"" Shane Ham, chief counsel for the Arizona attorney general's Elder Abuse and Complex Litigation Unit, told ABC News. Jones' team, according to Ham, ran an ""equity stripping"" scheme and aimed to be at a homeowner's door within 20 minutes of foreclosure documents being filed with the county.
""Equity stripping is when fraudsters will try to convince a homeowner to sell their home at far below the fair market value,"" Ham said. ""They do that by convincing the homeowners that they are there to help them, when in fact they are there to take ownership of the house.""
Jones' attorney declined to comment to ABC News, but wrote to the court that the Arizona Attorney General's Office's case is flawed, saying there was no scheme and that what Jones did ""is not 'equity stripping.'""
The Arizona Attorney General's Office has pursued the case against Jones and his co-defendants in civil court, not criminal court.
When ABC News asked Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes whether anyone could face criminal prosecution, she said in part, ""I don't want to foreclose that possibility,"" noting that their investigation has not concluded.
""For me, and I think for most of the families that we've talked to, the most important thing is to get their money back to them, to get their homes back to them, and to do it as quickly as humanly possible. And that's what we're trying to do,"" Mayes said.
Advocates and law enforcement officials say people who are struggling to make their mortgage payments should watch out for those who say they can help, and instead turn to their state's attorney general's office, a licensed real estate agent, or a nonprofit real estate legal services provider.
""Do not let one of these fraudsters in your house. Slam the door on them if you have to,"" Mayes said.
There are calls to change laws, both in Arizona and in states around the country, to help prevent homeowners from being taken advantage of.
Proposals have included tightening regulations targeting people without real estate licenses, assessing penalties against any lawyers and title companies who might be involved in foreclosure schemes, and specifically banning ""equity stripping"" in order to provide recourse for homeowners.
""I just wish that the police and the court services and the government would just really tighten up on this type of thing,"" Lewry said.
In both Lewry's case and Polizzi's case, homes were lost after they say promises were made.
""It's a nice neighborhood we're going to. It's quiet,"" Polizzi said of the area on Staten Island where her new rental home is located. ""But it's not home. And I don't know if it'll ever feel like home.""
24/7 coverage of breaking news and live events"
92,2026-07-29,"ByJohn Koetsier,
Senior Contributor.
The FCC has moved to block new Chinese-made advanced robotic devices, including humanoids and quadrupeds, and connected power inverters from the U.S. market. These items were added to the ""Covered List"" due to national security concerns, citing potential supply chain vulnerabilities, cybersecurity risks, and data collection by foreign powers. This decision effectively bars new models from receiving necessary FCC authorization, significantly impacting Chinese manufacturers who currently dominate the global humanoid robot market. FCC Chairman Brendan Carr emphasized securing critical supply chains. While existing models can still be sold, this forward-looking ban creates a protected market for emerging U.S. robotics companies, though it may also limit competition. Conditional approval remains a possibility for firms demonstrating no unacceptable risks.
The Federal Communications Commission moved to block a new generation of Chinese robots from the U.S. market, adding ""advanced robotic devices"" such as humanoid robots and quadrupeds to its Covered List of gear deemed too risky to authorize for sale in the United States. The same order added connected power inverters, the networked devices that route electricity through solar arrays, batteries and data centers.
While not entirely unexpected — robots, like self-driving cars, are mobile sensor packages that could theoretically be used by foreign powers – FCC Chairman Brendan Carr framed the move as an economic one as well.
""Following President Trump's leadership, the FCC will continue to do our part to secure America's critical supply chains,"" FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement, adding that the agency was ""acting in lock step with our national security agencies.""
The practical effect is clear: new foreign-produced robots and inverters are now generally barred from receiving the FCC equipment authorization that nearly every electronic device needs before it can be imported, marketed or sold in the U.S. Without authorization, foreign robot makers have essentially no market access.
It’s still very early days in the humanoid robot market, but the most recent brand share data we have shows that Chinese companies are shipping more than any other, with six of the top 10 manufacturers based in China, collectively responsible for  87% of all shipped humanoid robots.
Here’s the data, from January of this year:
It’s important to remember, however, that multiple American companies are very close to shipping in volume, especially 1X with Neo, Figure with Figure 03 (and Figure 04 coming soon), Agility Robotics with Digit v4 in the market today and Digit v5 coming in December, and Apptronik with Apollo. 
All of these are strong contenders, and there are many more. I’m currently tracking 180 companies in the U.S. that are working on 160 robots or components, like arms and hands. These companies have raised huge war chests totaling $150 billion.
Now these companies will have a protected market in which to grow. However, they will also be sheltered from competition to some degree, perhaps retarding the pace of their improvements.
Essentially, this blocks the current market leaders – all Chinese robotics companies –  from shipping to the most lucrative market on the planet.
It helps to be precise about the scope. The Covered List designation applies to new device models seeking authorization. It does not claw back robots or inverters that consumers already own, and it does not stop retailers from continuing to sell, import or market models that cleared the FCC’s equipment authorization process before Tuesday. In other words, this is a forward-looking gate on the U.S. market, not a recall. A company with an already-authorized quadruped can keep selling it. A company with a brand-new humanoid it hoped to launch this fall cannot … unless it wins an exemption.
Practically speaking, however, the best robots are tomorrow’s robots. Being limited to today’s models will kill any nascent market share foreign companies have in the United States.
The FCC order also carves out the federal government entirely: Washington can keep buying and using these devices.
The order allows for ""Conditional Approval,"" granted by the Department of War in the case of robots and the Department of Homeland Security in the case of inverters, for devices found not to pose an unacceptable risk. For any robotics firm hoping to keep U.S. distribution, that application process just became very important.
Hopefully, political contributions will not play a role in these decisions.
The FCC did not reach this conclusion on its own. Under the Secure and Trusted Communications Networks Act, the commission can only update its Covered List at the direction of national security authorities. 
The trigger here was a determination by a White House-convened interagency body that these foreign-made products, ""regardless of the nationality of origin,"" pose ""unacceptable risks to the national security of the United States or the safety and security of United States persons.""
Two risk categories drive the finding. 
The first is supply chain: a dependence on foreign-produced hardware that could be disrupted in ways that ripple through the U.S. economy and critical sectors. The second is cybersecurity. 
On robots, the determination is striking in how physical it gets: ""The networked capabilities of advanced robotic systems create extensive vulnerabilities and vectors for attacks that can manipulate the data and physical operation of the advanced robotic system."" Advanced robots, it continues, “collect data that could be leveraged by malign actors to surveil Americans, enhance the capabilities of foreign intelligence services, or to remotely commandeer the robots.”
In recent months the FCC has taken similar Covered List steps against Chinese-linked drones and their critical components, as well as consumer-grade routers.
Although the FCC’s fact sheet names no company, the market math points in an obvious direction: China.
China dominates the humanoid and quadruped categories, as you can see in the market share data above, and the clear leaders are Unitree, Agibot and UBTech.
Unitree launched commercially in Europe on July 22, becoming the first Chinese-made humanoid to reach Western commercial markets, and industry watchers had flagged a planned North American push in the weeks ahead.
The FCC’s conditional approval path leaves some ambiguity. 
How quickly will the Department of War and DHS process applications? What evidence clears the bar? A firm that can demonstrate secure, auditable supply chains and locked-down firmware might find a route back in.
For most of the American public, nothing changes today. For Chinese humanoid robot companies, this basically redraws the map."
93,2026-07-29,"In 2025, an electronic eye implant surfaced that promised to restore vision for some patients suffering from age-related macular degeneration. Named the photovoltaic retina implant microarray, or PRIMA for short, it combines a subretinal voltaic chip implant and augmented reality glasses — they work in tandem — to restore sight for those who have otherwise lost it. At the time, it was undergoing regular studies, but it has since passed clinical trials, and is now launching commercially. It has been proven to help patients with geographic atrophy caused by age-related macular degeneration.
It is now officially the first-of-its-kind ocular implant to be approved, and the first to receive a ""CE"" marking for form vision restoration under the European Union Medical Device Regulation. The CE mark means that a product or device has received approval and meets European health, safety, and environmental protection standards. In this case, PRIMA will now be authorized for commercial availability and sale in European countries. Form vision restoration involves reading letters, numbers, and words, meaning practical vision is restored for those using the technology. From here, the first commercial implant will be available in Germany; meanwhile, PRIMA will see country-specific reimbursement applications and clinical site activations across Europe.
Science Corporation, the company responsible for PRIMA, is purportedly working with the Food and Drug Administration in the United States to bring the implant to market there. It has already received breakthrough device designation and humanitarian use device designation from the FDA.
The PRIMA implant and the augmented reality glasses restore what's called functional central vision. That's the straight-ahead or forward sight available to anyone with 20/20 vision that allows you to see things like fine details, which ultimately helps you read. The PRIMA system is meant to address geographic atrophy specifically, which currently affects 8 million people around the world. It's a form of progressive damage caused by dry age-related macular degeneration, where cells in an extremely light-sensitive area of the eye — a smaller region of the retina called the macula — slowly die off and do not regenerate.
Doctors implant the PRIMA chip beneath the retina in a patient's eye, and it works with the augmented reality glasses (pictured above) to restore vision through a built-in camera. That camera captures imagery of what the patient is looking at, processes it, and sends it to the chip via near-infrared light. Because the human eye cannot detect infrared, that signal won't interfere with normal vision in the patient. The chip or PRIMA implant then takes that light and converts it into electrical pulses to stimulate bipolar cells in the retina, and the signal is then effectively interpreted, passing a clear image to the patient.
It works differently than something like a robotic eye which promises to improve sight, but amazingly, it is a sort of self-powered implant system that never needs to be charged. The implant actually generates electricity directly from light, not unlike solar panels, which means patients don't need a power source tethered to it."

95,2026-07-29,"


Cool Hunting®















The three-row flagships debut in Brooklyn with adaptive beams that shadow oncoming drivers and seriously thin taillights
Audi unveiled its Q9 and SQ9 in Brooklyn and while the pair stands as the German brand’s largest SUVs to date, the more consequential story sits at either end of the bodywork with light is its through line. The Q9 family introduces Digital Matrix LED headlamps to the US for the first time on any Audi, a technology held back for years by federal regulation, alongside third-generation curved OLED taillights the brand positions as an industry first. The design philosophy treats illumination as both ornament and safety apparatus, capable of masking glare for oncoming drivers, projecting turn signals onto the pavement and rendering a hazard warning directly into the taillight. Performance follows in kind, but our eye goes to the light.
For Audi, illumination has long functioned as signature and safeguard at once, and CEO Gernot Döllner returned to that pairing during our conversation: “I believe that innovation in lighting is a big and important story because it adds character to the design of a car, plus it adds functionality that is highly safety related.”
The Digital Matrix LED adaptive driving beam is new to the US market though hardly new to Audi. The brand developed and launched the system in Europe back in 2013, but the Department of Transportation rules kept it off American roads until 2022. Audi’s engineers spent the interval since then bringing the hardware in line with the stricter DOT standard. Conventional high beams have two states: on and off. The Digital Matrix system works in gradients, drawing on a micro-LED array of 25,600 individually addressable points. Front-facing cameras read oncoming traffic in real time and the array darkens only the precise pixels that would blind another driver, holding full brightness across every other surrounding degree of the road.
The Q9’s third-generation curved digital OLED taillights use a glass substrate just 0.1 millimeters thick—roughly the width of a human hair—bent across three dimensions to follow the body’s contours. The curve is more than sculpture. By wrapping the panel around the vehicle’s edges it widens the angle from which the lights stay legible, a subtle geometric gain that lets a driver approaching from off to the side register the car sooner.
That same segment density does more than sharpen visibility—it hands the driver a measure of authorship over the car’s face and tail. Six panels carry 512 individually controlled segments, enough resolution that the lighting becomes programmable. Through the MMI interface a driver selects from eight signature patterns spanning both the daytime running lights up front and the taillights behind, each configuration subtly broadening the vehicle’s apparent width. And when the moment demands it the rear segments turn instructive—when sensing a hazard, or engaged by the hazard switch, the OLED resolves into a warning triangle set into the taillight itself, addressing whoever follows behind.
The lighting innovations also extend to the ground. The Q9 and SQ9 cast a stylized turn signal onto the pavement beside the vehicle, synchronized to the car’s dynamic indicators—most vivid after dark though still somewhat legible in daylight. The projection signals to cyclists, pedestrians and motorcyclists near the car and also reaches drivers one lane over, whose view of the fixed indicators might be blocked by a vehicle between them.
Strip away the horsepower and the seat count and what Audi has really built is a vehicle that treats its own surface as a display—one that shadows an oncoming driver, widens its stance on command, throws a signal onto the asphalt and, when the situation turns, warns the car behind in plain pictographic language. Döllner is candid that the appeal is finally emotional: “good lighting gives you a good feeling, and I believe that’s very important, and it also is an important business case”. The Q9 doesn’t just illuminate the road. It reads it, and answers back. Both the Q9 and SQ9 reach dealerships in the fourth quarter of 2026.
Audi’s Q9 and SQ9 Make Their Case in Light

            Chinese demand for outbound travel continues to grow in 2026 despite war and diplomatic fallout. What's holding the market back now isn't hesitation, it's a shortage of flights.        
Select a question above or ask something else
The biggest constraint on China’s outbound travel isn’t demand — it’s the availability of flights, according to a Dragon Trail Research survey of 310 Chinese travel agents to be released Wednesday.
More than three-quarters of agents said demand for outbound trips grew in the first half of 2026 compared with a year earlier. A similar share said summer bookings were also up, a strong sentiment in a year the industry expected to be difficult.
The clearest proof of the issue with flights is Japan. After a diplomatic dispute in late 2025, China cancelled roughly half of all China-Japan flights. Yet Japan came in second among the “most noteworthy” destinations for 2026, and third for summer bookings.

A Dragon Trail Research survey of 310 Chinese travel agents finds that the primary bottleneck for China's outbound travel in 2026 is not consumer demand but flight availability. More than three-quarters of agents reported year-over-year growth in outbound demand and summer bookings, defying expectations of a difficult year. Despite severely constrained air capacity, key destinations remain popular: Japan ranked second for noteworthy destinations even after China cancelled roughly half of China-Japan flights (capacity at 48% of 2019 levels), Thailand topped both rankings thanks to visa-free entry and value (capacity at 54%), and the U.S. ranked fifth despite flights at just 27% of pre-pandemic levels. The market is also diversifying toward younger (18-35), independent travelers from Tier-1 and Tier-2 cities. Meanwhile, AI adoption among agencies has surged to 99%, though usage remains shallow and constrained by data-security and reliability concerns. The core industry question has shifted from whether Chinese travelers will return to whether airlines, visa offices, and airports can scale up to meet demand that already exists.

99,2026-07-29,"Yan Diomande joined RB Leipzig in July last year
After an eventful couple of months, Yan Diomande, one of football's top young prospects, is about to get his wish of joining a European powerhouse.
The 19-year-old is signing with Real Madrid in what can only be described as a record-setting transfer.
The Spanish giants will pay more than Â£100m for Diomande - making him a new record sale for RB Leipzig and he is expected to also become a new record signing for Real Madrid.
Simultaneously, Diomande becomes the most expensive African player in history.
His rise to stardom has also occurred seemingly in record time as less than 18 months ago he was almost completely unknown.
Only scouts and insiders were aware of a skinny winger who had previously played for Florida-based DME Academy, more renowned for its basketball programme.
Diomande had trials with Chelsea, Bournemouth, Crystal Palace, Rangers and Olympiacos but remained unsigned until he was picked up by La Liga side Leganes.
Within only a few months and 10 games in the Spanish top flight he increased his market value considerably, eventually signing with Leipzig for 20m euros.
The Red Bull-funded club believed they had acquired a diamond. And they were right. Almost from the get-go, Diomande looked like one of the most exciting players in the Bundesliga, scoring 13 goals and providing 10 assists across competitions in his first full season in Europe.
Initially, Leipzig intended to keep him for another year, hoping his market value would increase even further, but the demand was simply too high.
Liverpool, Paris St-Germain and finally Real Madrid showed so much interest in the winger, who also played for his home country of Ivory Coast at the World Cup, that the Bundesliga side had to give way and agree to a deal.
A lot can be said about the steadily increasing prices on an overheated transfer market where even prospects can demand fees in the nine digits, but Diomande is special. A hyper athletic and very creative winger, it is not far-fetched to consider him among the best dribblers in Europe already.
Obviously, there is still a degree of rawness to his game, but he made a leap forward in the past 12 months and there's no reason he couldn't develop further at the tender age of 19.
His passing skills are also somewhat underrated because he is the embodiment of a dribble-first winger. Diomande connected very well with right-back Ridle Baku at Leipzig and may look even stronger with the support of the likes of Trent Alexander-Arnold and Marc Cucurella.
Diomande is able to play on both wings. A right-footer, he was mostly employed on the left side by Leganes but found a home on the right at Leipzig. At the World Cup, he played in both positions for Ivory Coast.
Diomande made his La Liga debut for Leganes against Real Madrid
Diomande is not just marketable as a living, breathing highlight reel, but also because he is a true rags-to-riches story.
Growing up in the metropolis of Abidjan in Ivory Coast, he decided early to become a professional footballer. Or at least he dreamed about it and was willing to make a lot of sacrifices.
When he was nine, Diomande played for Inter Foot Sud Comoe, a club near the border to Ghana - 70 miles away from home. He essentially lived on his own and had to get by with few resources.
He made an even more drastic move when he left Ivory Coast at the age of 15 to play football in the United States, attending a high school while unable to speak English at first.
His talent was undeniable, yet there was no shortage of stumbling blocks on his way to professional football.
Numerous trials in Europe went nowhere. ""Even the B teams in the MLS didn't want me,"" he later said. ""I didn't even know why. They never gave me a reason. The adults handled everything. They just kept taking me all around Europe, and everybody kept saying no.""
Once his visa expired and he had to go back home, his fortunes turned around, as Leganes were ready to sign Diomande to a professional contract.
As things were finally on the up, he had to cope with an unexpected tragedy when his 15-year-old sister, who he had been very close with, died shortly after his La Liga debut against Real Madrid at the Bernabeu.
""Now, I don't feel anything. It's like I'm not even human. Since you died, I'm just blank,"" he wrote in a letter to Roxanne, his late sister, published by Players' Tribune in June.
Diomande said his dream of debuting against Madrid at the age of 18 was suddenly followed by a nightmare.
The circumstances of Roxanne's death remain somewhat unclear â Diomande was told that it was a result of her drink being spiked at a party.
Diomande scored 12 goals in 33 Bundesliga games last season 
While tragedy struck in spring 2025, it didn't derail Diomande from his professional path. He made the move to Leipzig a year ago and is already taking another huge step by joining Madrid.
The Spanish giants aren't in need of another winger, but Diomande is such a fascinating prospect that chairman Florentino Perez and Madrid's management did not want to lose out.
That Diomande ends up in Madrid rather than Paris is a surprise, though. PSG have been a club Diomande admired as a kid, with his dad being a supporter of the 14-time French champions.
Real Madrid can certainly be considered a dream destination for most footballers, but the way an anticipated move to PSG fell apart and how PSG voiced their displeasure over the behaviour of Diomande's agents can raise questions.
While outstanding on the field, there has been considerable noise around Diomande off it and his decision to switch professional representation has led to a complaint being lodged with the Court of Arbitration for Sport in Lausanne.
He had previously been represented by Maxidel, an agency led by Max Gradel, a former winger with the third most international caps for Ivory Coast, but Diomande then made the move to Jay-Z's entertainment agency Roc Nation in February.
Understandably, Roc Nation had great interest in signing Diomande and even sent out DJ Khaled to convince him of the move. Maxidel was not amused and insisted on the validity of previous agreements.
Under Fifa rules, agencies are allowed to sign players to contracts that make them the sole representatives in a country like England for a specific period of time.
It might not be a coincidence that Roc Nation represents two Real Madrid attackers in Vinicius Junior and Endrick already.
While it seemed as if PSG were in the pole position, the European champions officially declared they were out of the race a few days ago, citing issues with the agency during negotiations.
As for Diomande, his upward trajectory looks set to continue in the Spanish capital.
